6 Macro overview – at a glance

Rajat Bhattacharya 
Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 10

Global Market Outlook 10 
ECB to hike once more  in December, then pause . The 
Euro area recovery is firming. GDP grew 1.2% y/y in Q2 2026, 
up from 0.6% in Q1, and the manufacturing PMI stayed robust 
at 52.4 in October. Consensus 2026 growth has been raised 
to 1.0% from 0.9%, and 2027 is unchanged at 1.3%. The main 
supports are resilient exports and German infrastructure and 
defence spending. However, cautious consumers and soft 
private investment limit the recovery, and energy costs are 
squeezing household incomes and business margins. 
Recession is not the central expectation. French fiscal stress 
and sustained high energy costs remain the key risks. 
Higher energy prices pushed Euro area headline inflation up 
to 3.8% y/y in September from 3.2% y/y in August, while core 
inflation rose to 2.5%  y/y. Consensus expects headline and 
core inflation to ease to 2% y/y and 2.4% y/y by Q4 2027, 
respectively. Encouragingly, energy has had limited impact on 
core prices, and longer -term inflation expectations remain 
stable at around 2.1%. Together, these should allow inflation 
to moderate in 2027. The main risk is that energy , and 
especially gas, prices stay high for longer than expected. 
The ECB held its deposit rate at 2.5% in October after raising 
it by 50bps this year . Inflation data continue to favour an 
additional hike in December. We expect an ECB rate hold in 
H1 2027, followed by a cut by Q3 2027 as disinflation returns. 
This policy path is more dovish than money markets , which 
are pricing more than 60bps of hikes over the next 12 months. 
China to provide targeted support to defend growth floor. 
China's growth momentum recovered in September. Both the 
manufacturing PMI (50.1) and the non -manufacturing PMI 
(50.2) moved back into expansion. The construction PMI rose 
above 50 for the first time this year as infrastructure spending 
started to take effect. Exports grew 36.3% y/y, pushing the 
trade surplus to a record USD  133.5bn. We expect growth in 
2026 to meet the lower end of the 4.5-5.0% target. Growth is 
increasingly bifurcated, though , with  technology firms and 
exporters doing well, while household demand stays weak. 
Prices are rising again, but only narrowly. Consumer inflation 
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, and producer inflation 
surged to 5% y/y, its highest since 2022, driven by energy and 
metals prices and concentrated in upstream sectors. With 
domestic demand weak and a high comparison base next 
year, the boost from energy prices is likely to fade in 2027. 
Policy is in ‘targeted easing’ mode. We expect a modest fiscal 
package to defend the growth floor rather than a large 
stimulus. The PBoC cut a key  rate by 25bps to 1.5% to fund 
‘Six Networks’ infrastructure projects and raised its relending 
quotas. A new interest subsidy on first -home mortgages 
provides limited support to demand. We expect no policy rate 
cut this year. There is still room to speed up fiscal spending, 
including by using CNY 500bn of unused local -government 
debt quota. If the PBoC switches to an overnight reverse repo 
rate as its policy rate, that would imply a de facto rate cut as 
the overnight rate is lower than the 7-day official policy rate. 
BoJ to hike at a  quarterly pace  until Q2 2027.  Japan's 
economy remains solid. Wages are growing above 4% , and 
the labour market is tight. The main headwind is high energy 
prices, which is slowing growth. Fiscal spending gives some 
support: the FY27 draft budget includes ambitious spending 
plans, alongside a proposed two-year cut to the consumption 
tax on food and a new five -year defence programme. 
However, this extra spending raises questions about fiscal 
sustainability. The BoJ unveiled plans to triple the pace of ETF 
sales from January 2027, potentially funding the tax cut. 
Price pressures now look firmly established. Headline and 
core inflation rose above 2% for the first time this year, and 
core-core inflation (ex-fresh food and energy) jumped to 2.4% 
and is expected to rise towards 3%  in Q1 2027 . Breakeven 
inflation rates have stayed above 2% most of this year. Real 
interest rates remain negative, hurting savers and pensioners. 
The BoJ held its policy rate at 1.25% in October, after raising 
it in September, only three months after its previous hike. This 
points to an accelerated quarterly hiking pace. We expect the 
quarterly pace of hikes to continue, taking the rate to 2% by 
June 2027. Money markets have caught up with our hawkish 
view in recent months. The BoJ noted for the first time that 
underlying inflation has reached its 2% target, marking a shift 
from creating inflation to containing it. Politics could slow the 
hiking cycle  in H2 2027. Two government -appointed board 
members voted against the September hike, and the two most 
hawkish members are due to leave in July 2027.

Global Market Outlook 11

Our view

Foundation: Bond yields remain near multi-year highs, offering an attractive entry point. We view fixed income as a Core holding 
(ie, a Neutral allocation) in our multi-asset portfolios. Within the asset class, we maintain an Overweight stance on EM USD 
government bonds, given their attractive risk-reward. We hold an  Underweight view on Developed Market ( DM) government 
bonds due to the tail risks associated with term premia. 
The Fed’s decision to hold rates at 3.75-4.00% in October amid still-elevated energy prices suggests that every Fed meeting 
from here is ‘live’, with a rate change possible on incoming data . With US inflation still running hot and the labour market tight, 
we expect two more 25bps  hikes (one by year-end and one in H1 2027), bringing the Fed’s target range for the federal funds 
rate to 4.25-4.5%. We continue to favour the 3 -7-year part of the  US government bond yield curve as the best hedge against 
any risk-off move driven by AI-related growth fears. As inflation pressures ease in H2 2027, we see room for the Fed to consider 
a rate cut, which should pull nominal yields lower. Solid corporate fundamentals and supportive technicals should keep spreads 
tight, but there is limited room for them to tighten further. We still favour EM USD over local-currency (LCY) government bonds. 
Opportunistic ideas: We are bullish on US TIPS, AAA-rated CLOs, broad US HYs and Australian dollar (AUD) corporate bonds.

DM rates – Underweight 
We remain Underweight DM government bonds. In the US, 
government bond yields have retraced meaningfully after a 
sharp bear steepening through September and early October. 
We expect real yield s to stay elevated over the next six 
months as AI capex and productivity growth continue to drive 
above-trend economic growth. We expect two more 25bps 
rate hikes by June 2027.  
Over the next few months, we expect US government bond 
yields to remain rangebound. The near-term path hinges on 
five factors: 1) the 3 November US mid-term election, where 
a ‘Democratic sweep’ would likely be bond -supportive by 
constraining US President Trump’s fiscal spending agenda , 
while a ‘Republican sweep’ would likely weigh on bonds as 
markets price in further fiscal expansion and a higher term 
premium; 2) Fed communication at upcoming policy meetings; 
3) the US Treasury’s quarterly refunding announcement on 4 
November, where a ny signal of reduced long -end issuance  
would likely compress long-end yields; 4) softening auction 
demand and repatriation by foreign investors, which add 
uncertainty around demand for US government bonds; and 5) 
the f luid geopolitical situation in the Middle East , which is 
keeping oil prices elevated, with a risk of further upside. 
Going into H2 2027, we expect moderating oil prices and the 
fading inflationary impact of tariffs to give the Fed room to 
consider rate cuts. The US 30-year yield, however, is likely to 
stay elevated as fiscal deficit remains substantial next year.  
We expect a final ECB hike to 2.75% by December 2026, then 
a pause in H1 2027, followed by a 25bps cut in Q3 2027. In 
Japan, we expect quarterly pace of BoJ hikes, with one hike 
that takes the policy rate to 1.50% by December 2026 and two 
more in H1 2027, reaching 2.00% before pausing at mid-year.

Global Market Outlook 12 
DM corporates – Core holding 
We maintain a Neutral allocation to both DM IG and DM HY 
corporate bonds, with a tactical bias to add exposure on 
episodic weakness, as carry continues to dominate total 
returns across DM credit. Credit spreads have widened from 
their lows but remain close to historical tights, while yields are 
still attractive, reinforcing a carry-led stance. This tight-spread 
regime is anchored by robust issuer fundamentals, strong 
profitability and resilient balance sheets, despite a visible 
moderation in rating upgrades over the past quarter.  
Technical supply dynamics are reshaping sector dispersion,  
especially in the IG space, where heavy AI-related issuance 
is weighing on the long end of the curve. AI -driven supply is 
surpassing USD 2 50bn in 2026 and mak ing up a growing 
share of US IG gross issuance, but there are growing signs 
that hyperscalers are turning to private markets for fundin g, 
which should help ease public bond market supply pressures.  
We remain selective at the long end  of the yield curve and  
favour a duration of  3-7 years, where risk -adjusted carry 
remains most durable. Issuance-driven concessions in 
sectors with strong capex cycles (notably technology, capital 
goods and basic industries) present opportunities for investors 
who can stomach near-term volatility. 
Fig. 14 DM IG spreads have widened from their lows, 
but remain near historically tight levels 
Bloomberg Global Aggregate Corp Index, option-adjusted 
spread (OAS)

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
DM HY spreads  have widened to roughly a six-month high. 
Persistent doubts about whether hyperscalers can generate 
adequate returns on massive AI capex investments triggered 
a broad risk asset sell-off. The CCC tier has become the focal 
point of genuine credit concern, with yields hovering near the 
high-15% range and spreads near a four-year high. Market 
focus has shifted to the funding pressures facing AI-related 
neocloud companies. We view this  as idiosyncratic for now 
and see a limited impact on the broader HY universe.  The 
underlying credit picture is mixed. Leverage is elevated and 
interest coverage is thin, but the broad market has not yet 
shown signs of systemic stress. The US HY d efault rate  
remains below trend in 2026. 
HY offers an attractive income proposition, with all-in yields of 
over 8%. Its shorter-duration profile, combined with its lower 
sensitivity to changes in the term premium and rate volatility, 
make it an attractive sleeve in the current phase of the cycle.  
We remain attentive to evolving credit rating migration trends 
and sector-specific supply pressures, particularly as upgrades 
slow and new issuance clusters around key themes. However, 
healthy issuer fu ndamentals and stable credit metrics  
continue to support it as a Core holding allocation. 
Fig. 15 DM HY spreads are off the tights 
Bloomberg Global HY Aggregate Corporate Index, OAS

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM government bonds – Overweight 
USD/Core holding for LCY 
We are Overweight EM USD government bonds and retain 
EM LCY government bonds as a Core holding, reflecting a 
balanced perspective across both regional performance and 
currency dynamics. In EM USD sovereign debt, the recent 
spread widening offered an attractive entry point to gain 
exposure to areas benefiting from resilient global growth and 
stable external conditions. Technicals remain favourable, with 
supply being front-loaded and investor positioning remaining 
relatively light compared with historical levels. 
Many EM central banks having tightened earlier and more 
aggressively post -pandemic, preserving credibility and 
maintaining positive real rates. External balances have 
improved, reserve buffers have increased and greater 
reliance on domestic funding reduces external vulnerability. 
Lula’s surprise win in Brazil’s presidential election raises 
political risk. The country’s fiscal trajectory under the incoming 
administration is a key risk to monitor. Fiscal slippage could  
trigger a broader repricing of LATAM risk. In Asia, higher-rated 
issuers continue to offer a valuable buffer during bouts of 
global rate volatility, while select frontier exposures in EMEA 
remain more sensitive to liquidity shifts and changes in risk 
appetite. Index composition also shapes resilience: the EM 
USD benchmark is less exposed to oil price shocks , as 
countries that are not oil importers account for a larger share 
of the benchmark than in  the LCY benchmark. We expect 
returns to be primarily driven by carry rather than further 
spread compression.

EM LCY debt faces additional headwinds, especially as 
elevated US real yields and an appreciating USD dampen 
demand from foreign investors and reduce the comparative 
attractiveness of broad -based duration strategies. However, 
real yields remain comparatively supportive in several 
markets, and current valuations signal selective opportunities, 
especially given the restrictive monetary policy stances 
prevalent across many EM economies. Accordingly, EM LCY 
bonds retain their Core holding status, with portfolio emphasis 
placed on country selection and measured duration exposure, 
reflecting the differentiated currency dynamics at play. 
Key risks to both  the USD and LCY segments include a 
potential escalation of the  Middle East conflict , higher oil 
prices, renewed hawkishness from the Fed that could support 
further USD strength and weather-related disruptions, such as 
the effects of El Niño . Climate risks could prolong inflation 
pressures and hinder disinflation in vulnerable EM regions. 
Asia USD bonds – Core holding 
We retain a Neutral allocation to Asia USD bonds, an asset 
class that continues to reward investors with attractive 
nominal yields, favourable supply -demand technicals and 
robust credit fundamentals. Healthy corporate cash flows, low 
leverage and a high share of sovereign-linked issuers lend the 
segment a defensive quality relative to broader credit. 
The resilience has been evident year to date (YTD). Asia USD 
bonds have outperformed  both DM government and DM IG 
corporate bonds  on a total -return basis. We expect this 
relative strength to persist. The combination of carry and 
credit quality (over 60% of issues are sovereign or sovereign-
related) anchors our conviction in the asset class, even as we 
maintain a Neutral stance.

Bond opportunistic views 
Bullish US HY bonds 
We are opportunistically bullish on US HY bonds. We expect 
HY corporate earnings and cash flows to remain solid and 
leverage to remain stable. US HY bonds’  shorter-duration 
profile offers lower sensitivity to interest-rate volatility, which 
is an attractive characteristic in an environment of elevated 
term premia. 
Bullish US inflation-protected bonds (TIPS) 
Real yields remain elevated versus historical standards , 
driven by rate hike expectations. Inflation concerns persist as 
energy prices remain subject to upside risks. We believe TIPS 
offer protection against near-term inflation uncertainty.   
Bullish AUD corporate bonds 
The threshold for further RBA hikes has risen, as higher 
energy prices weigh on the growth outlook and complicate the 
policy trade-off. The transmission channel matters too, where 
higher long -end yields could feed through to the property 
market, tightening financial conditions without the RBA having 
to act, casting further doubt on the case for additional hikes. 
Technicals have improved slightly as higher yields slow new 
supply. Supply remains a tail risk, though. The AUD bond

market is much smaller than the USD market, especially at the 
long end of the curve, where demand for duration is thinner 
and the term premium is most sensitive to supply. 
Bullish AAA-rated CLOs 
Concerns about spillovers from p rivate credit are mounting 
amid deteriorating covenant protections in parts of the 
leveraged credit market. However, we believe AAA-rated 
CLOs backed by solid asset portfolios and substantial credit 
enhancements underneath senior tranches should help 
dampen credit risk and volatility. We see a compelling case 
for floating-rate and shorter -duration alternatives as portfolio 
diversifiers amid elevated term premia.  
Close utilities corporate hybrids 
AI capex is set to keep rising, but funding it has become 
harder. Tighter financial conditions now coincide with growing 
competition for capital. Utilities' credit fundamentals remain 
strong. Even so, we close our utilities corporate hybrids idea 
to lock in a gain of 0.5% as a risk-management step.

### Page 14

Global Market Outlook 14

Our view

We downgrade global equities from Overweight to a Core allocation as we expect higher volatility in the near term. We 
expect this volatility to arise as equity market leadership broadens during the transition to a mid-cycle bull market. Equity returns 
will likely moderate from the 15-20% annualised range of the last few years but remain positive. We remain Overweight US and 
AxJ equities, which are expected to deliver the highest earnings growth in 2027 among the major regions. 
Within AxJ, we remain Overweight Taiwan, given structural AI-driven growth, and upgrade India to Overweight, for diversified 
non-AI growth exposure. South Korea is a Core holding, with attractive valuations but extreme volatility . China is also a Core 
holding, with attractive valuations but lacking a positive catalyst. We are Underweight ASEAN, which has a weaker growth profile. 
We maintain Japan as a Core holding as nominal growth supports broad-based earnings revisions. We also maintain a Core 
allocation to Europe ex-UK, which is enjoying an earnings recovery. We remain Underweight UK equities, given the market’s 
limited exposure to growth sectors.

### Page 15

Global Market Outlook 15

Shifting to a mid-cycle bull market  
The last few years have seen strong annual returns of 15-20% 
for global equities , consistent with the early cycle of a bull 
market, driven in large part by a rapid AI buildout. As the AI 
infrastructure buildout is increasingly debt funded  – we 
estimate 40% of AI capex in 2027 would be debt -financed – 
the market is increasingly concerned about the quality of this 
growth and execution risks . W e remain positive on the  
structural AI theme, but we expect investor focus to shift from 
rapid buildout to monetisation . During this transition, we 
expect market leadership to broaden  beyond technology. 
We believe this shift will also be accompanied by higher near-
term volatility and a moderation in global equity returns , 
consistent with a transition to a mid-cycle bull market.  
Fig. 18 Projected 16% earnings growth for global 
equities in 2027 provides a strong tailwind for positive 
equity returns  
2026 and 2027 consensus earnings growth estimates for 
MSCI AC World sectors

Source: FactSet, Bloomberg, Standard Chartered  
US equities – Overweight  
US equity valuations have declined this year, with the 12-
month forward P/E falling to 19x from 22x at the start of the 
year. This reflects higher rates, a valuation headwind, a nd 
worries about the quality of growth, which is increasingly debt-
funded. As market leadership broadens and the US mid-term 
election nears, we expect higher near-term volatility in the US 
market. Nonetheless, US equities have broad-based strength, 
with the ongoing Q3 earnings season point ing to a robust 
economy. In a world of rising geopolitical risks, the US is also 
relatively insulated by its domestically driven growth. 
AxJ equities – Overweight 
AxJ has the strongest earnings growth  among major 
markets, as its technology sector remains a key beneficiary of 
the AI capex cycle. AxJ’s P/E valuations have also dropped to 
attractive trough levels, the lowest among major regions. 
Within AxJ, we are Overweight Taiwan, which benefits from 
clear visibility on AI -driven growth and dominance in 
manufacturing advanced AI chips. We also upgrade India to  
Overweight due to its resilient domestically driven earnings, 
providing diversification beyond AI-related growth. India offers 
the highest exposure to defensive sectors (healthcare, 
consumer staples and utilities) within AxJ.  Both Taiwan and 
India offer attractive returns on equity.  
South Korea equities remain a Core holding. We are positive 
on long-term agreements that improve earnings visibility for 
memory chipmakers. Valuations are attractive  (the lowest in 
AxJ), with supportive corporate governance reforms . Still, 
extreme volatility remains the key overhang. China remains 
a Core holding , given inexpensive valuations , but re-rating 
potential appears delayed amid AI moneti sation struggles, 
lingering regulatory risks and weak domestic demand. We 
remain Underweight ASEAN , as Indonesia’s downgrade 
warning, oil shocks and a well-supported USD could weigh on 
the region, although Singapore offers defensive growth. 
Fig. 19 India offers highest AxJ exposure to defensive 
sectors (healthcare, consumer staples and utilities) 
Sector weights for MSCI equity indices

Source: FactSet, Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
Japan equities – Core allocation 
Rising nominal growth is supporting solid broad-based 
earnings revisions , with rising rates supportive of bank 
earnings. Corporate governance reforms also continue to 
boost shareholder value . The fiscal support for strategic 
industries is pro-growth, offsetting energy shocks.  
Europe ex-UK equities – Core allocation  
Europe ex-UK is seeing an earnings recovery after three 
stagnant years, and we expect to see a continuation of this 
narrative with Q3 results. Rising rates are supportive of banks’ 
earnings and fiscal expansion is benefiting domestic demand. 
The fiscal situation in France has no easy solution, but we do 
not expect a contagion widening in government bond spreads, 
as the ECB has tools in place to limit contagion.  
UK equities – Underweight  
The UK lacks exposure to growth sectors, with relatively 
muted earnings growth. We expect this to weigh on the UK, 
making it lag the performance of other markets over the next 
12 months, although UK equities tend to hold up better during 
bouts of volatility.

Gold outlook:  We remain constructive over 6 -12 months, 
viewing the latest pullback as liquidity-driven rather than  a 
deterioration in fundamentals. USD strength is a headwind , 
but gold’s sensitivity to real yields has eased amid continued 
central bank  buying, led by China , and resilient investor 
demand. Speculative positioning near May lows raises scope 
for renewed inflows. High oil prices sustain near-term volatility 
but may eventually lift gold if they constrain global growth.

Oil outlook:  Crude prices remain elevated as shipping 
disruptions drive freight costs to multi-year highs, sustaining a 
geopolitical risk premium . While Middle East exports have 
proven resilient, threats to production infrastructure are 
mounting. Refined products , especially diesel , face the 
greatest supply pressure amid depleting inventories and 
constrained refining capacity. With limited emergency supply 
buffers, oil markets remain vulnerable to prolonged disruption.

15 Gold, crude oil – at a glance

Anthony Naab, CFA

### Page 18

Global Market Outlook 18

USD view

USD consolidation with limited upside: Our three-month US Dollar Index (DXY) forecast stands at 101, revised upwards from 
100.20 last month, reflecting a higher expected Fed rate path. With the October Fed rate pause and a potential December rate 
hike largely priced in, further tightening would offer limited additional USD support. Persistent inflation, elevated US government 
bond yields and resilient business activity continue to underpin the USD's interest -rate advantage. However, in September, 
payroll growth slowed sharply to 29,000 (from 133,000 in August), the unemployment rate rose to 4.2% from 4.1%, annual wage 
growth eased to 3.0% from 3.1% and housing starts fell 5.2% m/m. These signs of cooling demand temper expectations of more 
aggressive Fed tightening. We expect DXY to remain rangebound between 101 and 103, with our three-month forecast at 101.00. 
Peaking Fed rates and narrowing rate differentials favour gradual USD weakness: We maintain our 12-month DXY forecast 
at 98.50. We expect a Fed rate hike in December 2026 and another in H1 2027, taking the federal funds target range to a peak 
of 4.25-4.50%, before easing in H2 2027 . Further BoJ normalisation and the ECB's relatively restrictive policy stance should 
progressively narrow the USD's rate advantage. Persistent fiscal and current account deficits increase reliance on foreign capital 
inflows, adding to medium -term pressure  on the USD . Resilient US growth, AI -related capital inflows and elevated US 
government bond yields remain important offsets, supporting DXY’s gradual decline towards 98.50.

### Page 20

Global Market Outlook 20

Summary

Our stock-bond model (3-6 months) is at -2 (minimum: -5) in October, signalling a modest Underweight (UW) in global 
equities. Historically, a decline in the model score to -2 has been associated with flat to slightly negative returns. The shift was 
driven by stretched valuations in both global and Asian equities,  weaker fundamentals and deteriorating market technicals. On 
fundamentals, market risk aversion has risen sharply, while PMI new orders have declined over the past three months, pointing 
to a slowdown in the pace of industrial expansion. On technicals, the share of equity markets trading above their 200-day moving 
averages, a key gauge of long-term support, has fallen below 60% for the first time since January  2025. The model rebalances 
in the middle of each month and has generated 11bps of alpha from the equity Underweight initiated on 15 October 2026, lifting 
YTD alpha vs. the 60/40 equity-bond benchmark to 4.0%.  
Our 1 -3-month equity market regime model indicates contained bear market risks across US and global equities . 
Estimated bear market probabilities are 4.2% for the S&P500 and 7.7% for the MSCI AC World, compared with 0.9% and 6.4% 
respectively in the September outlook. Across both models, volatility measures are the main factors keeping probabilities low, 
supported by momentum signals. Together, these outweigh modest upward contributions from macro and cross-market factors, 
leaving little evidence of a bear market regime over the next 1-3 months.  
Our market diversity indicator flags reversal risk in MSCI Europe Real Estate and MSCI China Industrials, which are down 
over 12% and 19% respectively from their August peaks. US equity and USD positioning remain broadly neutral.

• Fed funds rate to natural rate ratio is still high 
at 1.7x, reflecting a restrictive monetary policy 
compared to historical average of 1.3x 
Key model 
factors 
• Economic activity, macro risk and surprise indices, 
corporate earnings, forward P/E ratio and technical 
factors 
• Conference Board leading indicator, Consumer 
Confidence Index, ISM New Orders and Fed 
funds rate 
How does it 
work? 
• A monthly scorecard of -5 to 5 based on 
fundamentals, valuations and market breadth 
factors to indicate the relative preference for bonds 
and equities. A positive score favours equities and 
vice versa 
• A probability model based on regression. Long-
term statistical tests are conducted to identify 
the four key economic factors above

Short-term Technical analysis Investor positioning 
Current views Bullish S&P 500 & MSCI AC World MSCI Europe Real Estate and China Industrials 
What factors 
is this view 
based on? 
• Bullish S&P500 and MSCI AC World. Bear market 
probabilities remain low at 4.2% and 7.7% 
respectively. Volatility measures are the main factors 
keeping these estimates low, supported by 
momentum signals and outweighing modest 
contributions from macro and cross-market factors 
• Market diversity indicator flags reversal 
risk in Europe Real Estate and China 
Industrials. The indices have declined by 
more than 12% and 19%, respectively, from 
their August peaks 
• Investor positioning remains broadly neutral 
across both US equities and the US dollar, 
indicating that investor exposure is not 
especially stretched in either direction 
Key model 
factors 
• Market factors: Momentum, volatility, interest rate 
differentials, relative returns, inflation swap rates, 
economic surprises, etc 
• Price action: Overbought conditions occur 
when prices rise sharply; oversold conditions 
happen when prices fall rapidly in a short time 
How does it 
work? 
• Scanning through 7,000+ factors, the framework 
uses ML to forecast market regimes or future trends 
based on identified market drivers 
• A market indicator based on fractal analysis 
that provides a timely indication of investor 
positioning based on price actions

Source: Standard Chartered

Global Market Outlook 26

Time is your most precious commodity – be sure to spend it wisely 
Time is undoubtedly valuable. The days may seem long, but the years are short. So, make the choice 
to spend your time wisely. Whether you’re setting out on your investment journey, navigating the 
intricacies of mid -life wealth planning or fortifying assets  for your golden years, invest time today to 
ensure your wealth strategy is aligned to what’s right for you – Today, Tomorrow and Forever. 
Setting aside the time now to review your plan will pay dividends in the future. Markets have moved. 
Your portfolio’s current asset allocation may no longer be optimally positioned to maximise the 
opportunities ahead. Ask yourself the following: Am I holding too much cash? Am I sufficiently allocating 
to growth assets for the long term? Is my portfolio diversified? Am I capturing the best opportunities? 
And most importantly, is my wealth working hard for me so that I don’t have to? 
Use our SC Wealth Select framework and advisory specialists to help guide you through this process.

Purpose 
Today,  
Tomorrow,  
Forever 
Our approach to helping you grow and manage 
your wealth starts with you. We use a goals -
aware approach to understand your vision of 
Today, Tomorrow and Forever for yourself, your 
family and beyond, and then design portfolios to 
meet your various needs.  
Using our ‘Today, Tomorrow and Forever’ 
approach, we ensure your wealth needs for the 
near term (Today) are met, while ensuring your 
wealth needs for the decades ahead (Tomorrow 
and Forever) are also planned for.  
Your vision of ‘Today, Tomorrow and Forever’ is 
unique to you. Our specialists partner with you to 
build well -diversified, long -term Foundation 
portfolios, aligned to your Today, Tomorrow and 
Forever needs. Opportunistic ideas are added to 
capture short -term opportunities, a nd sufficient 
protection is included to address the objectives of 
you and your family. 
Today, Tomorrow and Forever 
Approach 
Planning for Today 
Requires ensuring liquidity and income 
flows take centre stage 
Securing Tomorrow 
Entails a well-diversified investment and 
protection portfolio with a focus on growth, 
ensuring inflation is accounted for and risks 
are mitigated 
Building for Forever 
Involves greater focus on long-term returns 
given the time horizon of your portfolio can 
be measured in decades, and might also 
include business interests, real estate, 
collectibles or charitable funds

SC Wealth Select  
 Managing your wealth through the decades – Today, Tomorrow and Forever

### Page 27

Global Market Outlook 27

Principles 
that stand the 
test of time 
Adhering to time-tested principles, to ensure your 
investment decisions remain robust and 
consistently applied, is paramount to your 
success Today, Tomorrow and Forever. We use 
five Wealth Principles to guide and guardrail your 
wealth decisions.

Discipline – ensure consistency and 
prudence over your emotions 
• Reacting to emotions, such as optimism and 
fear, can lead to poor investment decisions at 
the worst times 
• Have a plan and stick to it – this helps you to 
stay focused on the bigger picture

Diversification – simply put, don’t put 
all your eggs in one basket 
• Reduce risk by holding a variety of financial 
assets. Multi -asset diversification in your 
Foundation portfolio is important 
• As a guide, make sure your portfolio contains 
a variety of asset classes and investments 
that have low correlation with one another

Time in the Market – a more robust 
strategy than timing the market

• Predicting market selloffs is challenging, and 
timing your exit and re-entry is difficult 
• Missing out on the best performing days of a 
market can have a significantly detrimental 
impact on your portfolio 
• ‘Time in the market’ and buying the market 
with a longer -term view provide more 
consistent returns that can ride out bumps 
along the way

Risk and Return – make sure the risk 
is worth the return 
• To achieve higher investment returns, you will 
likely have to accept a greater level of risk in 
your portfolio 
• Therefore, it’s important to understand the 
risks and manage these on an ongoing basis

Protection – don’t let the unexpected 
catch you unprepared 
• Even though you may feel healthy, or 
financially stable now, protection offers the 
ability to overcome times of financial 
uncertainty and mitigate the long-term impact 
of unforeseen events on your wealth 
• A good protection plan not only safeguards 
your wealth today, but also considers the 
value of your future earnings over your 
lifetime, in today’s terms

Advisory 
Process 
Following a 
holistic 
approach to 
managing  
your wealth 
We follow a rigorous process to ensure your 
needs and objectives are well -understood, and 
your portfolio is well-aligned and manage s to  
deliver on these objectives. 
However, markets constantly evolve and your 
needs change. Hence, we encourage you to 
undertake regular portfolio reviews to ensure your 
portfolio remains aligned to your Today, 
Tomorrow and Forever objectives. This proactive 
approach includes strategic reb alancing based 
on insights from our Chief Investment Office.

## Document 440: US Midterms_ The cost of living is now a pivotal issue (2026-11-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/US Midterms_ The cost of living is now a pivotal issue (2026-11-02).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026EDITORIAL
US MIDTERMS: THE COST OF LIVING IS NOW A PIVOTAL ISSUE
1 AP-NORC, October 2026.
2 Jared Bernstein, Daniel Posthumus, ‘The way we were: Price-level shocks and consumers’ memories’, SIEPR, May 2026.
3 72% of registered voters rank ending the war with Iran as one of the top priorities for the next Congress, including 60% of Republicans (AP-NORC, October 2026).

4 Oleg Korenok, David Munro, Jiayi Chen, ‘Inflation and attention thresholds’, GLO Discussion Paper Series, No. 1175, Global Labour Organization, 2022.
5 Rajashri Chakrabarti, Thu Pham, Beck Pierce, Maxim L. Pinkovskiy, ‘Tracking the K-shaped economy: who’s driving spending?’, Federal Reserve Bank of New York, Liberty
Street Economics, 1st May 2026.
6 The year 2020 is excluded due to the disruptions caused by lockdowns.
In the United States, growth is holding up, unemployment remains low and consumer spending shows no sign of slowing.

On paper, that should flatter the president’s party ahead of the midterms. Yet, Republicans could lose control of Congress.
The rise in the cost of living, which weighs on households far more than macroeconomic aggregates suggest, could be one
of the reasons. On top of persistent inflation, rekindled by the war in Iran, there is also a slowdown in wage growth and
rising housing costs. The election could hinge on affordability, yet whichever party wins will lack the means to address it.

A change in the majority in Congress will nonetheless have an impact on the country’s economy.
On 3 rd November, Americans will elect a new Congress for the next
two years. With Republicans currently in control of the White House,
the House of Representatives and the Senate, the balance of power in
Washington could shift. Polls suggest that the House, at least, could
swing to the Democrats. Beyond the new balance of power, the vote
will reflect how voters view the way the country is being run. Here, we

focus on the cost of living.
INFLATION: THE INCUMBENTS’ BURDEN
The incumbent party goes into the election with two handicaps.
The first is structural: no matter who holds the White House, the pre -
sident’s party almost always loses seats in midterm elections. The
second stems from the combination of robust but uneven growth
and persistent inflation, which is undermining household confidence.
Pre-election polls1 confirm this assessment: the economy and the cost-

of-living top voters’ worries.
Inflation has overshot the Fed’s target every year since 2021. Prices are
up 28% over five years: 31% for housing (not including mortgage rates,
which have more than doubled), and 29% for food. This shock is felt all
the more keenly as it is the most severe in forty years and follows on
from decades of low inflation2. Furthermore, it hits the lowest-income
households the hardest. In addition, the election comes after an ener-

gy-driven rise in inflation. Energy accounts for the entire gap between
the latest CPI reading (3.7% y/y in September) and the last figure re -
corded before the conflict in the Middle East started (2.4% y/y).
Fuel has made things worse. The rise in the price of a gallon (46%
between February and October) caused by the Iran war — which is
unpopular even amongst Republican voters3 — is an aggravating factor.
In 2026, inflation only slightly crossed the ‘attention threshold’, the

3.6%y/y rate beyond which households start to regard it as problema -
tic4. Fuel is a recurring, unavoidable expense whose price is directly
observable, unlike many items in the CPI basket. This results in a subs-
tantial gap between perceived inflation and measured inflation.
Inflation must be viewed alongside the other drivers of households’
well-being. Today, wages, payrolls and the housing market are moving
the wrong way for most Americans.
IT'S NOT JUST PRICES

The 2022 midterms suggest caution in reading the polls. Democrats
defied expectations of a ‘Republican wave’, retaining control of the Se-
nate and losing the House by just a few seats, even though inflation
averaged 8% that year. This reminds us that inflation must be viewed
alongside the other drivers of households’ well-being. Indeed, how
people experience the cost of living depends on the interplay between
prices, wages and housing. In 2022, wages, payrolls and the housing

market were all growing faster than in 2026. Today, all three are mo -
ving the wrong way for most Americans.
Wage growth is slowing just as inflation picks up again. In September
2026, average hourly earnings were up 3% y/y in nominal terms, the
slowest rate since 2018, and now below inflation. Real wage earnings
are therefore falling (-0.7% y/y in September, with six out of nine mon-
ths showing a decline in 2026). The rise in the cost of living is thus

compounded by a net loss of purchasing power. The Atlanta Fed reports
that wage growth is lower for the bottom quartile of earners (the 25%
of employees with the lowest pay) than for the top quartile (the 25%
with the highest pay): 3.8% y/y vs. 4.4% on average since late 2024,
following 10 years in which the gap favoured the bottom quartile. Top
earners also benefit most from the wealth effect linked to stock mar -
ket valuations. This explains the resilience of aggregate consumption.

However, this rests on a relatively narrow base, mostly households
earning more than USD 125,000/year5. Meanwhile, a falling saving rate
(3.8% in September 2026, down 1.4pp y/y) suggests households are
drawing on their savings to offset price rises. A large share of the po -
pulation is therefore excluded from the growth that has been observed.
Beyond wages, there is the question of employment prospects.
The unemployment rate remains low and consistent with full employ-

ment. Yet the labour market looks stagnant: low firing despite repeated
shocks and low hiring despite solid growth. Payroll growth (an average
of 68,000 per month since January) is well below that of the previous
decade (nearly 200,000 per month between 2011 and 2019 6). Com-
bined with a workforce that is no longer growing due to an ageing
population and a restrictive immigration policy, this is enough to keep
the unemployment rate low and the labour market looking healthy.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
But households are likely to be suffering from a lack of opportunities,
and consequently from a lack of prospects for wage growth that would
help them to increase their purchasing power. The Conference Board’s
labour differential, a gauge of perceived job availability, says as much:
outside the pandemic, it is at its lowest since 2015.
Housing is the area that has deteriorated the most. The rise in

mortgage rates that began in 2022, from under 4% to over 7%, has
dampened the property market. This is true on the demand side, as
expected, but also on the supply side. The sharp rise in interest rates
is deterring homeowners with low-interest mortgages from selling, as
they would have to borrow again at current rates. This ‘lock-in effect’
prevents prices from falling despite weaker demand. The lock-in effect
is also affecting tenants, as the decline in the availability of housing is

driving up rents. Housing lies at the heart of the disconnect between
the macroeconomic picture and voters’ perception of it, as they face a
multifaceted rise in the cost of living. That rise is also driven by credit:
monthly repayments on a home of the same value have risen shar -
ply. This additional cost, which inflation figures do not capture7, erodes
households’ purchasing power in the property market.
A CONGRESS POWERLESS ON THE COST OF LIVING

The next Congress will, in all likelihood, have little room for ma -
noeuvre. Neither party is within reach of the thresholds required to
override the filibuster (which allows 41 out of 100 senators to block
a bill from coming to a vote) and the presidential veto (two-thirds of
both chambers).
Affordability is likely to be a key issue in the election, but the winners
will lack the tools to address it. The Democrats’ agenda relies notably
on the reintroduction of schemes that were abolished or scaled back

under the current administration (Affordable Care Act tax credits, Me-
dicaid). Even if Congress passed them, they would be subject to a pre-
sidential veto. There is no question of direct support for households or
a restrictive fiscal policy.
Whether inflation falls will depend on two factors: how the conflict in
Iran unfolds and the effectiveness of monetary policy. The labour mar-
ket should hold its current balance: robust growth (see our EcoFlash)

is supporting demand, whilst failing to alleviate supply-side pressures,
and the unemployment rate could continue to fall. Household senti -
ment will continue to depend on how prices develop. As for housing,
it is unlikely to improve in the short term. With the 10-year Treasury
yield still at 4.8% at the end of 2027 (BNP Paribas forecast), 30-year
mortgage rates would, at best, remain around 7%.
THE OTHER ECONOMIC STAKES OF THE VOTE
Historically, divided governments have brought legislative stability,

which is viewed favourably by the markets. But polarisation has in -
creased in recent years and is likely to feature prominently in the bud-
get vote and the debt-ceiling debate. The debt limit, in particular, could
be reached as early as Q1 2027, depending on the Treasury’s borrowing
requirements. The current administration, meanwhile, has taken major
decisions (on tariffs, immigration and Iran) without involving Congress,
a practice that is likely to continue.

7 Marijn A. Bolhuis, Judd N. L. Cramer, Karl Oskar Schulz, Lawrence H. Summers, ‘The cost of money is part of the cost of living: new evidence on the consumer sentiment
anomaly’, NBER Working Paper, No. 32163, 2024.
Risks to the deficit remain tilted to the upside. According to our base -
line scenario, it is expected to remain stable at 6.3% of GDP in 2027,
but the post-election balance of power could add to the cost of the war
in Iran and rising debt-service costs. In particular, interests could align

in favour of increased spending should the Democrats gain control of
both houses of Congress. Avoiding, or ending, a shutdown could require
a trade-off involving mutual concessions from the presidency on key,
popular aspects of the Democratic agenda (such as healthcare), and
from the Democrats on defence.
The AI sector is exposed to the vote. The economic stakes are high
given how much the associated investment cycle and the wealth effect
contribute to US growth. However, at this stage, the risk is not legisla-

tive. As with the cost of living, sweeping legislation is unlikely, all the
more so with a president holding a pro-AI stance, and who holds the
right of veto. The risk lies rather in sentiment and further out. A Demo-
cratic sweep could lead to increased congressional activism on regu -
lation, on concentration in the AI industry, and on the knock-on effects
on the cost of living (data centres and electricity) and on jobs. Putting
these issues on the agenda could create a climate conducive to tighter

regulation in the medium term and weigh on the sector’s valuations.
Furthermore, the mid-term elections will see 36 governors’ seats up
for election. These governors have extensive powers (regarding autho-
risations and incentives) when it comes to the establishment of data
centres in their states.
Finally, the midterms will matter for monetary policy. The Senate
confirms, by simple majority, the president’s nominees to the Fed’s
Board of Governors. Consequently, should the Senate come under De -

mocratic control, President Trump would need a consensus pick to re-
place Governor Powell from 2028 — or to fill any other seat that might
become vacant during the next Congress.
*****
Whatever the outcome of the election, disinflation and, more broadly,
relief from the cost of living, depend less on Congress than on the
White House and, to a lesser extent, the Fed. The key factor lies in the
resolution of the conflict in Iran, coupled with an effective monetary

policy to enable a sustainable return to price stability.
Anis Bensaidani
anis.bensaidani@bnpparibas.com

## Document 451: What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 19 / 21 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA-SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P ., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P . Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints

and struggles in implementing the programme in practice within each

State aid is still the main vehicle for this. In response to the 2021–
2022 energy crisis, the European Union temporarily relaxed the rules
on this type of aid in order to support businesses facing rising energy
prices. Any intervention of this kind, provided that it is selective and

could affect trade within the single market, must be authorised in ad -
vance by the European Commission in principle. Since June 2025, the
Clean Industrial Deal State Aid Framework (CISAF) has provided a more
sustainable framework for these kinds of interventions. In particular,
this framework, which runs until the end of 2030, authorises Member
States to grant temporary aid to electricity-intensive businesses to off-
set the cost of electricity. The aid must be targeted and come with

decarbonisation commitments12. Against the backdrop of the crisis in
the Middle East, the temporary METSAF scheme, adopted in April 2026,
helped (in relation to measures falling under the CISAF) to increase the
proportion of additional costs offset by governments from 50% to 70%,
thereby offering additional support to the most at-risk sectors.
GERMANY IS STILL SUFFERING FROM INSUFFICIENT IN-
VESTMENT IN ITS ELECTRICITY NETWORKS, WHICH THE
AID SCHEMES ARE ATTEMPTING TO OFFSET

The issue of electricity costs is a particularly hot issue in Germany,
given the importance of industry to the economy (around 20% of va -
lue added, one of the highest levels in Europe) and its exposure to
international competition. Furthermore, the delay in modernising
electricity infrastructure (only 22.6% of the high-voltage lines planned
for 2025 had actually been completed by that date) is causing bottle -
necks between the production areas in the north and the consump -

11 European Court of Auditors, 2026, “Implementation of the REPowerEU plan must be stepped up”.
12 It applies to the most electricity-intensive activities, which are seeing their competitiveness threatened by international competition, and follows the 4x50 principle: 50% of
electricity consumption is eligible for a subsidy; Of the eligible proportion, the subsidy may not exceed 50% of the wholesale price;
The final wholesale price paid by the company must not be less than €50/MWh;

50% of the aid received must be reinvested in decarbonisation (renewable energy generation, storage and improvements in energy efficiency); Around 90 sectors are covered by this
reform, accounting for approximately 2,000 companies, including in the chemical, steel and paper industries. The scheme, which is strictly regulated, will run for just three years
(from 1st January 2026 to the end of 2028) at an estimated total cost of between EUR 3 and 5 bn, financed by the special Climate and Transformation Fund.

13 Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2015) and Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2025). These costs mainly cover the costs of physical infrastructure; operational,
regulatory and compliance costs; and costs associated with using the transmission and distribution network.
14 The Industriestrompreis (industrial electricity price) is the public subsidy mechanism aiming to reduce the price of electricity for some industries.
15 The indirect carbon cost compensation mechanism is a government support measure for electricity-intensive industries, which aims to offset the cost of carbon-emission

allowances for energy producers.
tion centres in the south. As a result, the costs of accessing services
have increased fivefold over 10 years (rising from EUR 1 bn in 2014
to EUR 5.2 bn in 2024 13). At the same time, as we have already seen,
taxation and grid charges are hitting industry hard, with levies on large
consumers far exceeding the European average despite some support
mechanisms being put in place for the most electricity-intensive com-

panies (such as the Bandlastprivileg measure to reduce grid tariffs).
As a result, German industrial production has suffered in particular
due to rising energy prices in recent years, including electricity, with
electricity-intensive industries being especially affected (Chart 8).
In order to cope with these higher electricity costs than the European
average, Germany has been using European support schemes to reduce
the cost of electricity for businesses, particularly since the Merz go -

vernment took office in May 2025. The main scheme — the Industries-
trompreis14, made possible by the CISAF — has been approved by the
European Commission for the 2026–2028 period. While it is less bene-
ficial than the indirect carbon cost (ICC) offsetting included in electri -
city prices15, it has a broader scope, meaning that businesses ineligible
for the ICC can benefit from it. Other measures complement this targe-
ted support by addressing other components of the electricity bill. As a

result, the electricity tax has been set at the European minimum for
manufacturing sectors, while the gas-storage tax has been abolished.
The government is also subsidising grid fees, which should halve them.
Overall, according to our estimates based on 2024 prices, the final
price of electricity for these industries could fall by around a quarter
(to 116 EUR/MWh for industries with annual consumption of between
70 and 150 GWh, compared with a European average of 123 EUR/MWh)

(Chart 9). Germany’s advantage over Central and Eastern European
countries, where electricity costs are generally already higher, looks
set to increase.

However, even when taking all of the subsidies into account, the price
of electricity in Germany would remain higher than the levels seen in
France, Spain or Portugal (and even more so in the United States and
China).
FISCAL MEASURES: LIMITED ROOM FOR MANOEUVRE
There are a number of limitations to these European schemes. For
the country implementing them, lower electricity costs mean that

its industry becomes more competitive against industries from other
EU countries, but this does very little to address the disadvantage that
it faces compared with the United States and China. Therefore, this
measure will unlikely be enough to counteract the loss of market share
seen vis-à-vis the United States and China. In addition, the support
schemes are restrictive (conditional on investment in the low-carbon
transition) and are transitional. Finally, their cost is partly passed on

to the public purse or to consumers who do not receive this support
(in Germany, the cost of the new energy price support measures is
estimated at EUR 14.3 bn in 2026, or 0.3% of GDP16).
Some European manufacturers have proposed abolishing or reducing
the carbon tax as a solution to getting electricity prices down quickly.
In Germany, this tax accounted for 14% of the electricity price in the
first half of 2025, which is higher than the EU average (11%). However,

this proposal is already covered by the indirect carbon cost offsetting
mechanism for electricity-intensive industries exposed to carbon al -
lowance prices. In addition, while adjustments are being made — no -
tably to slow the rate at which allowances are reduced — it seems
highly unlikely that the scheme will be significantly challenged. It is
one of the cornerstones of Europe’s policy to combat climate change,
which has been effective thus far, as shown by the falling greenhouse

gas emissions from industry. More broadly, the gradual introduction of
a carbon border adjustment mechanism (CBAM) should mitigate com-
petition distortions caused by environmental practices17.
IN GERMANY AND THE REST OF EUROPE, HEAVY INVEST -
MENT IS DRIVING UP ELECTRICITY BILLS IN THE SHORT
TERM
In response to the challenge of the low-carbon transition and indus -
trial competitiveness, European governments must strike a balance
between decarbonising the electricity mix and ensuring a supply of

energy that is flexible enough (particularly from controllable sources)
to guarantee stable electricity systems. The integration of an increa -
sing proportion of intermittent renewable energy sources requires
massive investment in transmission and storage infrastructure (statio-
nary batteries). This expenditure, which places a long-term burden on
grid costs, reduces the scope for support schemes (particularly public
ones) to address competitiveness gaps. In Germany, the cost of the

transition is estimated at between EUR 122 bn, according to the go -
vernment (2.8% of GDP), and EUR 200 bn, according to the DIHK18 (4.6%
of GDP), by 2030–2035. If we take the ECB’s assumption19 that govern-
ments will cover around 17% of these investments, the annual public
16 The Industriestrompreis is estimated at around EUR 1.3 bn per year, the subsidy for network charges is projected to amount to EUR 6.5 bn in 2026, the abolition of the gas sur -

charge around EUR 3.4 bn, and the reduction in the electricity tax nearly EUR 3 bn per year. Furthermore, the ICC had cost EUR 2.3 bn in 2024 for costs incurred in 2023.

expenditure burden of the transition in Germany would be between
0.5 and 0.8 percentage points of GDP by 2030. However, this budgetary
pressure comes at the same time as three other budgetary increases:
rising social expenditure linked to an ageing population; the necessary
increase to defence budgets to meet NATO’s target of 3.5% of GDP by
2029; and the rising cost of debt-servicing20.
Germany has already made significant progress in maximising its use

of renewable energy, putting it at the head of the pack in Europe. The
country generates 20% of Europe’s renewable electricity (solar and
wind) and accounts for 30% of installed storage capacity (BESS) 21, far
ahead of other European countries (ranking third globally). However,
despite this momentum, Germany’s dependence on gas has not yet
decreased. As a matter of fact, it is only partially offsetting the planned
closure of coal-fired power stations and the phasing out of nuclear

reactors. Furthermore, the intermittent nature of solar and wind power
could prolong this dependence in the short and medium term. In order
to ensure that the German electricity system is flexible, new invest -
ments in gas-fired power plants, supported by public subsidies, are
planned22.

SUBSIDIES ARE HELPING TO ADDRESS THE EMERGENCY
CAUSED BY ENERGY CRISES WITHOUT RESTORING THE
INTERNATIONAL COMPETITIVENESS OF ENERGY-INTEN-
SIVE INDUSTRIES
In response to the impact of energy crises on industrial competitive -
ness, European governments are finding themselves having to strike a
delicate balance between providing short-term support to industries

exposed to increased international competition and continuing — if
not, accelerating — energy transition policies which will result in hi -
gher costs for electricity-network users for several years to come.
Beyond direct economic support measures, which are difficult to sus -
tain long term, four priority areas are emerging:
1/ Demonstrating flexibility in implementing European directives in
order to factor in national specificities and the differing timeframes

between energy crises (which can lead to irreversible industrial conse-
quences) and reforms (which have a longer-term horizon). The relaxa-
tion of the European carbon tax mechanism 23 appears to be a step
towards this.
2/ Continuing efforts to decarbonise the electricity mix, which are still
the main vehicle for sustainably reducing electricity prices for Euro -
pean industries in the medium and long term.
3/ Optimising the use of electricity networks on the demand side, in

particular through smart grids, a flagship element of the Commission’s
COM (2026) 600 reform presented last July24.
4/ Coordinating European electricity networks in order to harness the
complementarities between national systems.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

## Document 474: 【國泰證期研究部】網通產業-調變未定通道先行400G電路將到來-20261029

Source PDF: all report pdf/【國泰證期研究部】網通產業-調變未定通道先行400G電路將到來-20261029.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-29
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1 
產業更新-網通產業 
調變未定通道先行，400G SerDes 將到來 
What’s New – 規格已定，訊號編碼方式未決 
資料中心交換器的埠速每隔幾年翻倍，方法是把每條線路的速度加倍，下世代的3.2T 需 8 條線路、
每條 400G，也就是 400G SerDes，即光互連論壇(Optical Internetworking Forum, OIF) 的 CEI-
448G。 該規格的框架文件已於2025 年 11 月發布 ，2026 年 2 月並啟動極短距與長距兩項子規格設
定，但這代首次遇到既有線路無法負荷，沿用現行PAM4 編碼需要線路承受 112GHz 頻率，但受連
接器限制只有約 90GHz，雖然沒有明言 PAM4 無法運作；但改用 PAM6 編碼可降到約 87GHz，代
價是訊號品質下降，且光訊號那端仍是 PAM4 須多一層轉換。兩者差距不只在頻率，階數越多則每
階間距越窄，糾錯負擔與晶片複雜度同步上升，關鍵在連接器能否再往上突破一成以上的頻寬，而
不在編碼技術本身是否有新解。 
Trends/Outlook – 三種線路方案同時出現 
速度越快 ， 銅線能傳的距離越短，標準規格的 112G 世代還有 2 公尺 、224G 世代降到 1 公尺 ，400G
世代連目標都未訂，除非以主動銅纜或繞過電路板的纜線設計可再延長。距離縮短迫使光學元件往
晶片旁移動，規格要求把晶片與到鄰近光引擎 的距離定在約 150mm，這正是近封裝光學 (Near-
Packaged Optics, NPO)的定義，意即 400G 電路就是 NPO 出現的原因，產業因而分三種方案。一
是拉高線路速度並換連接器以延續可插拔，NVIDIA 與思科的 USFP 對上 Arista 與 MSA 的 XPO，
前者單模組容量 12.8Tbps、頻寬與前面板密度為現行 4 倍，後者的多源協議成員半年內由 25 家增
至逾 150 家；二是把光學搬到晶片旁或封裝內的 NPO 與共同封裝光學(CPO)；三是 Credo 的寬而
慢，以數百條慢速光通道取代少數高速通道。 
Investment Summary 
1、 400G SerDes 的導入時程將呈現晶片先到、周邊生態系後到 的狀態，預期 博通支援 400G 
SerDes 的交換器晶片 TH7 將有機會於 1H27 問世，28 年則有配合 TH7 的第 4 代 CPO 模組
推出，乙太網聯盟的次世代晶片同樣列於 28 年，主機端的 PCIe Gen8 規格亦預計 28 年才完
成。224G/1.6T 或 224G/800G 仍是 26 與 27 年各廠商的主要貢獻獲利來源；未來 448G 的
瓶頸不全是因為晶片本身，尚有連接器、基板材料與主機端介面等週邊生態系需完備。 
2、 對台廠而言，將來為了導入 400G SerDes 架構，無論選擇 USFP 或 XPO，交換器前面板、連
接器與液冷模組都須重新設計，與晶片商的整合能力更為重要，同時可滿足 CSP 客戶在電訊
號與光通訊的需求，具 Tomahawk 平台開發能力的智邦 (2345 TT) 兩者皆可承接；貿聯 -
KY(3665 TT)既發表電訊號的 448G 互連方案，又是 Credo 寬而慢生態系整合商，為唯一同時
站上兩條路線的台廠。目前瓶頸落在被動零件與材料，受惠順序應由連接器、線材與高階基板
材料端向週邊網卡與交換器擴散。

訊號編碼與線路條件的取捨 
單一通道傳輸速度要提高，通常往兩種方向發展，一是每秒送出更多次訊號，二是每一次訊號多
帶幾個位元 。 早期乙太網到每通道25G 至28G 為止採用不歸零編碼(Non-Return-to-Zero, NRZ)，
訊號只有高低兩種狀態、一次帶一個位元；從 56G 世代起改用四階脈衝振幅調變 (Pulse 
Amplitude Modulation 4-level, PAM4)，把訊號分成四個高度、一次帶兩個位元，送出次數因而
減半，這正是過去三個世代能沿用既有線路的原因。到了 400G 世代，PAM4 與 PAM6 的差異如
表一與圖一所示，階數越多，每秒送出的次數與線路要承受的頻率越低，但每一階之間的間距也
越窄、越容易判讀錯誤。PAM6 還多一個麻煩，六不是二的整數倍，必須用兩次訊號傳五個位元
的方式折衷，編解碼複雜度明顯高於 PAM4，這也是它遲遲無法定案的原因之一。

圖一、NRZ / PAM4 / PAM6 Signalling at 448 Gbps

資料來源：OIF-FD-CEI-448G-01.0，國泰證期研究部 
編碼方式最後是由線路條件決定，OIF 指出目前 448G 線路的頻寬上限只有約 90GHz，主要卡在
連接器；晶片封裝端反而不是問題，封裝接腳間距1.0mm 對應 58GHz、0.65mm 對應 90GHz、
0.5mm 對應 115GHz，而0.5mm 以下已可量產 ， 意即封裝本身已能支撐PAM4 所需的 112GHz，
真正的瓶頸在連接器與各段轉接。訊號衰減的要求也同步收緊，56G 世代允許 30dB、224G 世代
為 40dB，400G 世代則要在更高的頻率下仍維持 40dB 以上，連帶使高階基板材料與連接器成為
能否成案的關鍵，而非晶片設計本身。表二也列出各級介面的耗電目標，從最短距離的 0.5 pJ/b
到最長的 2.5 pJ/b，距離越遠越耗電，這也說明縮短電的傳輸距離本身就是省電手段，並且是後
面評估寬而慢路線時應有的比較基準。

資料來源：OIF、Ethernet Alliance TEF 2025，國泰證期研究部整理 
訊號品質不足的部分可以靠糾錯編碼 (Forward Error Correction, FEC) 補回來，但能補的幅度有
限。OIF 明言現行的糾錯能力已不足以支撐 448G，必須再加一層額外編碼。華為在 OIF 研討會
提出的比較顯示，把糾錯能力從 6.9dB 提升到 7.8dB，代價是額外負擔由 5.8%升至 12.1%、運
算複雜度增加近五倍；而 PAM6 本身就需要約 12%的額外負擔，等於在起跑點就先付出一筆成
本。換句話說，PAM6 在頻率上省下來的，有相當一部分會在糾錯這項功能還回去。 
從晶片實作來看，PAM4 要做到 400G，類比電路的頻寬必須加倍、雜訊與抖動要改善一倍，製
程得推進到 2 奈米以下；PAM6 雖因送出次數較少而在部分電路上較省，但等化與判讀電路的複
雜度分別增加約五成與一倍 ， 綜合下來只略優於PAM4， 省下的電力大致剛好用在額外的糾錯上 。
結論是電與光最好維持同一種編碼，以免每種架構都要多一層轉換；PAM6 的技術風險較低，但
PAM4 在光的那一端與抗干擾上明顯較好，最終要由模組、線性驅動與銅纜三類設計共同決定。
值得注意的是，OIF 已明確表示業界對折衷速率並無需求，意即 400G 電路沒有中間站，產業必
須一次跨過這道門檻，沒有緩衝的速度可選，採用時程因此格外嚴謹。 
USFP、XPO 與寬而慢 
既然上限來自連接器，400G 電路的架構分流實際上由連接器進度決定。Amphenol 在 OCP 峰會
把 224G 之後的瓶頸依序排為晶片封裝出線第一、可插拔介面第二；TE Connectivity 則表示現行
OSFP 規格沒有通往 448G 的明確路徑。這也解釋了標準目標與實際系統的落差：規格中的 1 公
尺，是電路板走線、兩個連接器與纜線合計落在約 40dB 損耗預算內的結果；若把纜線直接接到
封裝、繞過電路板出線，同樣的預算就能讓給纜線本身。NVIDIA 的 NVL72 機櫃即屬此類，背板
以逾 5,000 條銅纜完成櫃內互連，總長超過 2 英里，而單條長度仍在 1 公尺以內，並未脫離同一
量級 。 四家主要供應商提出兩類互不相容的解法 ：Amphenol 與鴻騰精密(Foxconn Interconnect 
Technology, FIT)保留現行的卡緣(Card edge)接法，靠收緊公差與縮小接點間距克服；TE 與立訊
(Luxshare)則改用兩件式接頭，以金屬外框把每一對訊號完全包覆。四種設計的差別，最後體現
在頻寬能撐到多高，也就是能否達到 PAM4 所需的 112GHz，這道門檻決定了整體架構的走向，
也決定了哪些供應商進得了這一代的名單，時間壓力因此落在被動零件端。 
表三、OCP 2026 各廠 448G 可插拔連接器方案比較 
方案/廠商 介面型式 頻寬 超出 PAM4(112GHz)餘裕 差動阻抗 資料性質 
224G 可插拔(Amphenol 範例) 卡緣 約 78 GHz 不足 約 85Ω 已量產出貨 
XPO 448G(Amphenol) 卡緣、公差收緊 約 123 GHz 約 10% 89~92Ω 推估曲線、展場 demo 
XPO 448G(鴻騰精密 FIT) 卡緣、新接點 約 118 GHz 約 5% 89~92Ω 模擬，原型待實測 
MegaLane(TE Connectivity) 兩件式、無殘段 約 126 GHz 約 13% 約 92Ω 模擬，TE 稱已有實測 
448G OSFP(立訊 Luxshare) 兩件式 約 111 GHz 幾近於零 92Ω 與 85Ω 併行 模擬，已有樣品

### Page 4

投資 顧問

4 
如表三所示，四個 448G 設計都能跨過 PAM6 所需的約 90GHz，但相對 PAM4 所需的 112GHz
餘裕只有 0 至 14GHz。換言之，PAM4 能否成立幾乎完全押在連接器能否再往上推一成以上，這
也是 OIF 遲遲不敢定案的原因；Amphenol 甚至為了達到 120 至 130GHz，XPO 可能必須犧牲
與舊規格的相容性。400G SerDes 的編碼，本質上不是訊號處理技術之爭，而是連接器這類被動
零件的機構與公差之爭，勝負取決於幾家連接器廠的製造精度。 
NVIDIA 與思科 2026 年 10 月 12 日共同發表的超高密度小型可插拔(Ultra-Dense Small Form 
Factor Pluggable, USFP)，是PAM4 路線的第一個商業化產品。 這是首款支援每條線路400Gbps
的模組，在與現行 OSFP 相同的外形內放進 16 條雙向通道、單模組容量達 12.8Tbps，未來可再
擴充到 32 條以上；相較目前每條 200Gbps 的模組，頻寬與前面板密度提升為四倍、能源效率約
1.3 倍，並可在不另外配置散熱迴路的條件下支援最高 100 瓦；相較之下，現行 1.6T OSFP 世代
單模組功耗約 40 瓦，卻已須外掛液冷籠座與冷板組件散熱，如圖四，USFP 等於把散熱責任收回
模組本身。兩家公司明言其光學設計是以簡化訊號處理來降低延遲與耗電，方向指向線性驅動可
插拔光模組(Linear Pluggable Optics, LPO) ，而 LPO 唯一可行的編碼正是 PAM4。生態系已與
TE Connectivity、Amphenol 合作機殼與連接器，並計畫以開放規格推出。值得注意的是 TE 表
示 USFP 採用其 MegaLane 接頭，正是四種方案中頻寬餘裕最高的兩件式設計，顯示NVIDIA 與
思科選擇以更換接頭型式來換取 PAM4 的可行性，而非在編碼上退讓，等於把這一代是否能導入
的成敗因素交給連接器供應商的製造精度。 
圖二、XPO (eXtra-dense Pluggable Optics) Connector, Cage and Plug Module 
資料來源：鴻騰精密(FIT)，國泰證期研究部整理 
圖三、USFP 12.8Tb/s per module        圖四、Current 1.6T OSFP liquid-cooled 2X8 cage

### Page 5

投資 顧問 
產業報告 
 2026/10/29

5 
與 USFP(Ultra-Dense Small Form Factor Pluggable) 正面競爭的是 Arista 主導、鴻騰精密與
Amphenol 同屬卡緣陣營的超高密度可插拔光學(eXtra-Dense Pluggable Optics, XPO)。該協議
2026 年 3 月成立時約 25 家成員，至 OCP 峰會已逾 150 家；Arista 揭露的模組規格為單模組
12.8Tbps、整合冷板可散熱至每模組 400 瓦，與 USFP 同屬 12.8Tbps 等級；Amphenol 揭露的
連接器規格則涵蓋 64 條高速通道、單一48V 供電、整合液冷並相容MPO-16 光介面。兩套方案
的根本差異在於，XPO 保留現行卡緣接法以沿用既有機構與散熱設計，改版責任落在模組端的小
電路板上；USFP 則接受更換接頭型式，以換取更高的頻寬餘裕。對台廠而言，無論最後收斂到哪
一方，前面板、機殼、連接器與液冷模組都必須重新設計，交換器的工程價值反而因此提高，具
Tomahawk 平台開發能力的智邦(2345 TT) 在兩套規格下都能承接設計與製造，不必在規格定案
前押注任何單一陣營。 
第三條路線乾脆不參加速度競賽，Credo 在 OCP 峰會以 Wider. Slower. Better.為題主張，光學
應該在可靠度、耗電與成本這三項原本屬於銅纜的強項上取勝，作法是用數百條慢速的微型發光
元件取代少數高速通道，把加速壓力留在電的那一端。其耗電論據是：銅纜加上晶片電路約需
5pJ/bit，微型發光元件則低於 2pJ/bit，其中紅光微型 LED 低於 1pJ/bit、可達 2 公尺，藍光低
於 2pJ/bit、可達 10 公尺，微型雷射低於 2pJ/bit、可達 50 公尺。不過這個比較的基準是銅纜方
案；若改與表二中 OIF 對線性介面訂的 2.5 pJ/b 目標相比，優勢會明顯收斂。以 NVIDIA VR200 
NVL72 機櫃估算，銅纜方案約耗 10kW、約佔整櫃用電 5%，改用微型發光元件約可降到 4kW、
省下約六成 ， 相當於每顆GPU 省 80 瓦 。 產品方面 ，Credo 已於 10 月 12 日發表 1.6T 主動光纜 ，
以約 200 條光纖承載、單端耗電約 12 瓦、可達 50 公尺，刻意採用最簡單的 NRZ 編碼而不使用
訊號處理晶片 ， 並內建備援通道 ， 預計2027 年量產 ； 其路線圖為2027 年 1.6T 光纜與 6.4T NPO、
2028 至 2029 年 3.2T 光纜與 CPO。生態系中發光元件由 ams OSRAM 供應，光纖與連接器涵
蓋康寧(Corning)等廠，整合商則包含 Credo、貿聯(BizLink)與正崴(Foxlink)，三家整合商中台廠
即占兩席，是寬而慢路線上最直接的台廠受惠位置，惟兩家目前皆屬組裝與整合角色，尚未切入
上游的發光與光學元件，單位價值相對有限。 
圖五、µEmitter-Based Optical Interconnect Roadmap 
資料來源：Credo，國泰證期研究部整理 
須強調三條路線並非互斥，而是對應不同的傳輸距離。規格定義的距離由短到長依序為：封裝內
晶片對晶片約 25mm、晶片到封裝內光引擎約 50mm、晶片到鄰近光引擎約 150mm、晶片到可
插拔模組約 250mm、同一塊電路板內約 50cm、跨板或跨機箱約 1 公尺。其中 150mm 這一級
正是 NPO 的定義，而120mm 的線性級別明確指定使用 PAM4。也就是說，400G 電路在壓縮銅
線可達距離的同時，等於直接在規格裡為 NPO 與線性驅動留下位置，預期 NPO 也將於 27 年底
至 28 年進入市場，貿聯-KY(3665 TT)則同時出現在 448G 高速互連與寬而慢這兩條路線上。

### Page 6

投資 顧問 
產業報告 
 2026/10/29

6 
主機端同步倍速，PCIe 世代為何決定網卡速度 
400G 電路的時程之所以能相對確定地落在 2028 年之後 ， 還有一個主機端的原因 。 網卡的工作是
把線路上收到的每一個位元搬進主機記憶體 ， 反之亦然 ， 因此主機介面的頻寬不能低於網路埠速 。
主機介面就是 PCIe，它的世代因而與乙太網埠速形成一對一對應，背後邏輯與前面的線路完全相
同：每一代把每條線路的速度加倍，也在同一代遇上編碼方式的轉折。以業界標準的 x16 插槽計
算，PCIe Gen5 每條線路 32GT/s、單向有效頻寬約 505Gbps，承載 400G 埠速還有 1.26 倍餘
裕；但面對 800G 只剩 0.63 倍、短缺約 37%。這不是設計餘裕不夠，而是結構性不足，這正是
Gen5 只能支撐到 400G 的原因，與設計優劣無關。 
表四、PCIe 世代與可承載乙太網埠速對應關係 
PCIe 世代 每通道速率 調變與編碼 x16 單向有效頻寬 可承載埠速 頻寬餘裕 代表網卡 規格發布 
Gen4 16 GT/s NRZ、128b/130b 約 252 Gbps 200G 約 1.26 倍 — 2017 年 
Gen5 32 GT/s NRZ、128b/130b 約 505 Gbps 400G 約 1.26 倍 ConnectX-7 2019 年 
Gen6 64 GT/s PAM4、1b/1b+FLIT+FEC 約 968 Gbps 800G 約 1.21 倍 Thor Ultra 2022 年 
Gen7 128 GT/s PAM4、1b/1b+FLIT+FEC 約 1,936 Gbps 1.6T 約 1.21 倍 — 2025 年 6 月 
Gen8 256 GT/s 待定 約 3,872 Gbps(雙向 1TB/s) 3.2T 約 1.21 倍 — 
2025 年宣布
預計 2028 年 
資料來源：PCI-SIG、Broadcom、AMD、Marvell，國泰證期研究部整理 
PCIe Gen6 把每條線路速度倍增到 64GT/s，並在同一代完成兩項與乙太網完全相同的轉折：編
碼由 NRZ 改為 PAM4， 並改用新的封包格式搭配糾錯 ，x16 單向有效頻寬因而提升到約968Gbps，
承載 800G 有 1.21 倍餘裕。這個對應關係可由實際產品驗證：博通 2025 年 10 月發表的業界首
款 800G AI 網卡 Thor Ultra，明確採用 PCIe Gen6 x16 主機介面；AMD 在 Helios 機櫃所列的
第二代 Pensando Vulcano 網卡同樣是 800G、 同樣走Gen6， 每顆GPU 配置三張 、 合計2.4Tbps。
兩款 800G 網卡都無法在 Gen5 上成立，也說明 800G 網卡必須與 Gen6 同時問世，兩者的時程
實為同一條，網卡無法在主機介面到位前先行推出。 
依同樣的倍增邏輯，PCIe Gen7 已於 2025 年 6 月發布規格，每條線路 128GT/s、x16 單向約
1,936Gbps，對應 1.6T 埠速同樣是 1.21 倍餘裕；Gen8 則規劃每條線路 256GT/s、x16 單向約
3,872Gbps、雙向約 1TB/s，對應的正是 3.2T 埠速，也就是400G 電路世代。Marvell 已於 2026
年 2 月在 DesignCon 展示運行於 256GT/s 的 PCIe 8.0 電路——在規格尚未完成、系統尚未成
形的階段就先展示晶片，反映的正是主機介面與網路介面必須同代到位的結構性約束。值得注意
的是，PCIe 的線路要穿過插槽、轉接卡與較長的板上走線，條件比網路側更嚴苛，因此它的編碼
方式同樣還沒定案，面臨與 400G 電路相同性質的取捨。 
把三個層面合在一起看，400G SerDes 電路能否成立不取決於單一元件的突破，而是編碼方式、
連接器頻寬與主機介面三者必須同時到位：編碼的取捨懸在連接器能否再推進一成以上的頻寬，
連接器的兩種路線已分別由 Arista 主導的 XPO 與 NVIDIA、思科的 USFP 代表，主機端則要等
PCIe Gen8 規格於 2028 年完成 。 而三者的時程並不同步 ， 呈現的是晶片先到 、 周邊生態系後到。
依通路訪查，預期博通支援 400G SerDes 的交換器晶片 TH7 將有機會於 1H27 問世，28 年則有
配合 TH7 的第 4 代 CPO 模組推出，乙太網聯盟的次世代晶片同樣列於 28 年，主機端的 PCIe 
Gen8 規格亦預計 28 年才完成。224G/1.6T 或 224G/800G 仍是 26 與 27 年各廠商的主要貢獻
獲利來源；未來 448G 的瓶頸不全是因為晶片本身，尚有連接器、基板材料與主機端介面等週邊
生態系需完備。受惠順序因而由連接器、線材與高階基板材料端向週邊網卡與交換器擴散，對應
台廠為貿聯-KY(3665 TT)與智邦(2345 TT)。

達發 6526 TT  ( 電子–半導體 ) 
光通發力，再達新高 
投資與評價 
1. 達發 3Q26 受惠光通與乙太網持續放亮，且商務耳機出貨暢旺，營收創
單季新高，獲利優於預期；預期 4Q26 光通與乙太網延續成長，淡季不
淡，營運為歷史同期新高。 
2. 單通道 100G 開始出貨，預期 27 年逐步放量，10G PON 切入 AI 資料
中心 OOB，新一代 PHY 同步貢獻，上修 26 年及 27 年營收預估，預估
26 年 EPS 21.76 元；27 年 EPS 30.18 元，營運創歷史新高。 
3. 目前達發股價為 707 元，約 23 倍 FY27E EPS ，位於近四年本益比參考
區間 20-50 倍偏下位置。考量光通高速成長，網通切入全新市場，維持
買進投資建議；惟受上下游供給緊缺，毛利率上升空間有限，將評價由
平均值 35 倍調至中間偏下 30 倍，目標價 900 元(30x FY27E EPS)。

重點更新 
光通與商務耳機出貨暢旺，達發 3Q26 營運創近四年新高 
達發 3Q26 光通憑藉產能與交期優勢提升市占，單通道 50G 出貨優於預期，
單通道 100G 開始小量出貨，乙太網受惠低軌衛星規格升級與交換器市占提
升，藍牙受惠旺季及高階專案出貨，營收季增 10.4%至 69.41 億元，創單季
新高。毛利率 49.3%優於原先預估之 49.1%，費用方面控管得當，營業費用
22.87 億元(+0.6% QoQ)低於原先預估之 23.35 億元，營業利益 11.34 億元
(+20.3% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 17.0%至 10.29 億元，EPS 6.15 元，營運創
近四年單季新高。

預期光通與乙太網延續成長，達發 4Q26 營運為歷史同期新高 
達發 4Q26 提前拉貨比重有限 ， 預期藍牙受惠商務耳機量產 ， 跌幅小於往年；
光通單通道 100G 逐漸放量，乙太網 2.5G PHY 與交換器延續成長，因此上
修營收預估 5.3%至 67.80 億元(-2.3% QoQ)，淡季不淡。 預估毛利率47.8%，
營業費用 21.60 億元(-5.6% QoQ)，營業利益 10.83 億元(-4.5% QoQ)，稅
後淨利 9.88 億元(-4.1% QoQ)，EPS 5.90 元，營運為歷史同期新高。

### Page 2

報告內容 
光通與商務耳機出貨暢旺，達發 3Q26 獲利優於預期 
達發 3Q26 光通需求強勁，在市場供給偏緊下，達發產能與交期相對穩定，單通道 50G 持續提
升市占，出貨優於預期，單通道 100G 亦開始小量出貨，50G TIA 並以獨立晶片形式出貨。乙太
網受惠低軌衛星客戶由 1G 升級至 ASP 較高的 2.5G PHY，輕管理型交換器需求增溫。寬頻以北
美成長最快，10G PON 比重逐步升高。藍牙則為消費旺季，電競及商務耳機等高階專案出貨增
加，3Q26 營收季增 10.4%至 69.41 億元(+23.3% YoY)，創單季新高。 
毛利率方面，DRAM 及 NOR Flash 今年漲幅達數倍，封測成本亦上升，產品漲價於 3Q26 貢獻
有限，惟產品組合優化部分抵減成本壓力，毛利率季減1.9 個百分點至 49.3%，略優於原先預估
之 49.1%。營業費用 22.87 億元(+0.6% QoQ)，低於原先預估之 23.35 億元，營業利益11.34 億
元(+20.3% QoQ)，稅後淨利季增 17.0%至 10.29 億元，EPS 6.15 元，營運創近四年單季新高。

### Page 3

光通成長淡季不淡，預期達發 4Q26 營運為歷史同期新高 
達發 4Q26 有別於傳統淡季，預期光通與乙太網延續強勁成長，其中光通單通道 100G 出貨增
加，為最大成長動能，乙太網則受惠2.5G PHY 持續導入 Wi-Fi 新客戶及新一代 10G PHY 量產。
寬頻隨北美設備商產品陸續落地 ， 並切入AI 資料中心 OOB 新市場 ， 藍牙則受惠商務耳機專案量
產，高階應用占比提升，預估淡季影響優於往年，加上下半年提前拉貨占藍牙音訊及寬頻營收皆
不到一成 ， 後續客戶庫存調整壓力有限 ， 上修4Q26 營收預估 5.3%至 67.80 億元(-2.3% QoQ)，
淡季不淡。 
毛利率方面，產品價格調整預期自 4Q26 開始反映，光通占比持續提升，惟調價依客戶及產品線
逐案協商，預期無法完全反映成本漲幅，且 NOR Flash 及封測成本持續走揚，下修毛利率預估
1.3 個百分點至 47.8%(-1.5ppts QoQ)。下調營業費用 2.1%至 21.60 億元(-5.6% QoQ)，上調營
業利益預估 13.2%至 10.83 億元(-4.5% QoQ, +127.1% YoY)； 稅後淨利9.88 億元(-4.1% QoQ，
+78.0% YoY)，EPS 5.90 元，營運為歷史同期新高。
